People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. I just really feel like dying is the only fair punishment for what I've put my family and my crew through. It's just this heavy weight on me all the time, you know? 

Kind of like when we have to haul those massive rolls of broadloom carpet up the stairs. We had this one job last year at an old Victorian house they were converting into apartments, and the staircase was so narrow. Four of us just sweating and trying to pivot on the landing. I kept scuffing the fresh paint on the walls, and the general contractor was this guy named Rick who just yelled the whole time. He wore these bright yellow suspenders every single day and parked his obnoxious red truck right on the client's lawn. It had this huge dent in the bumper. 

But yeah... I just feel like I deserve it. The punishment, I mean.

Like I just said, it sounds exactly like a real person. When I hear him say, "You missed it," or "Everyone knows," it is so clear, I swear someone is standing right there directly behind me. But then I turn around, and nobody's there. 

It really messes with my head because it sounds just like one of the guys on the crew. We used to have this guy, Tommy, who would sneak up on people on site. He thought it was hilarious. He wore these heavy steel-toed boots but somehow he walked so quiet. Those boots are expensive, though. I usually get mine at this specialty workwear place out on Route 9. They have this digital machine that measures your arch, which is super important when you're on your feet all day on concrete subfloors. Concrete is just terrible for your joints. I've been wearing heavy-duty gel knee pads since I was in my twenties, but even then, the cold just seeps right through your jeans in the winter. We did a warehouse job in January once and my hands were so numb I could barely hold the utility knife to cut the seams. 

But yeah... it sounds completely real until I look and see that I'm totally alone.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, um... no, he doesn't say that. It's just what I said a minute ago. I'll be in my kitchen or on a job site, and I hear him right behind me saying, "You missed it," or "Everyone knows." It's so clear, like he's standing right on my heels, but when I spin around... nobody. Just me. 

But when he says "everyone knows," it really gets under my skin. Because, you know, when you're laying floor and you mess up a seam, everyone *does* know. You can't hide a bad seam, especially not on a patterned broadloom. If the pattern match is off by even a fraction of an inch, the whole room looks crooked. We had this one client, this lady with a huge house out by the lake, who ordered this custom geometric carpet. Took eight weeks to ship from Europe. The rolls were unbelievably heavy. My lower back was just screaming by the end of the week. I actually had to start seeing a chiropractor after that job. Dr. Allen. His office is right next to that old bowling alley on 4th street. Have you ever been there? They still have those old manual scoring sheets and the carpet in there is just... well, it's a biohazard at this point. It's this neon galaxy print that hasn't been deep cleaned since 1998. The padding underneath must be completely disintegrated into dust by now. 

But yeah, the voice just says those two things mostly. Not the punishment stuff.

Well, it's... I know exactly what it means. It's about the jobs. Even on the days when I'm not, you know, completely down in bed and crying all day... I just know it. I get this absolute certainty that the homeowners and the suppliers are all tracking me. They're using the floor patterns, the text messages, even the calls to the city for permits... they're using all of it to monitor whether I'm ruining the jobs on purpose. And my crew tries to tell me I'm crazy, but no one can talk me out of it because the proof is right there. 

Like the permits. Getting a permit pulled downtown is a nightmare. The building department is on the third floor of this brutalist concrete building that looks like a prison. The elevators never work. You have to walk up these stairs with these weird, shallow steps that totally throw off your stride. It's a trip hazard, honestly. If they had to pass their own safety inspection, they'd fail immediately. I took a picture of the stair nosing once to show my brother because he's a safety inspector for the county. He works mostly out in the rural areas, checking agricultural buildings. He had to go to a dairy farm last month and said the smell was just unbelievable. Stuck in his truck upholstery for a week. He drives this Ford F-150, the older model, before they switched to the aluminum bodies. The steel ones were so much better for hauling heavy loads, like rolls of carpet or bags of thinset. Thinset is incredibly heavy, fifty pounds a bag, and you need so many of them for a large tile job. 

But yeah, they're definitely using all that stuff to track me. That's what he means. Everyone knows.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah... yeah, there was. Actually, just a couple of months ago, there was this stretch of, I don't know, several weeks where the fog kind of lifted a bit. I was actually getting out of bed without it feeling like lifting a hundred pounds, eating a little better, not just crying all day long. But... even then, I still heard him. I'd be at work, feeling okay, and there he was, right behind me. And I was still completely convinced that the clients and suppliers were tracking me. 

It's funny how suppliers work, though. You'd think they'd want to make it easy for you to buy their stuff, but the supply house we use for the high-end adhesives is only open from 7 AM to 11 AM. It's ridiculous. The guy who runs the counter, Stan, he's been there since the eighties. He still uses a dot-matrix printer for the invoices. Have you ever heard one of those things go off? It sounds like a machine gun. It's so loud you can't even hear yourself think. They have this coffee pot sitting right next to it that looks like it hasn't been washed since the Clinton administration. It's got this thick brown crust on the inside of the glass. I don't know how anyone drinks it, but the tile guys from the union shop down the street are always in there downing it by the quart. Those guys are crazy, though. They work at this frantic pace, just slapping down thinset like there's no tomorrow. You can't do that with large format tile. You get lippage, and then the whole floor is ruined. You have to back-butter every single piece and use those leveling clips, the little plastic wedges. They leave little plastic bits everywhere when you snap them off the next day. 

But yeah, even when I was feeling better and not so depressed, the voice was still there, and I still knew they were tracking me.

He always said Texas had better barbecue, but I think he just wanted warmer winters. Anyway, yeah... I just feel like I deserve to be punished.\n\nDoctor: Are you thinking about killing yourself right now?\n\nPatient: Like I was saying, it's just... I keep thinking it would be so much better to just not wake up. I don't know if I'm about to do something right this exact second, but the thought doesn't really go away. I just really feel like dying is the only fair punishment for what I've put my family and my crew through. It's just this heavy weight on me all the time, you know? \n\nKind of like when we have to haul those massive rolls of broadloom carpet up the stairs. We had this one job last year at an old Victorian house they were converting into apartments, and the staircase was so narrow. Four of us just sweating and trying to pivot on the landing. I kept scuffing the fresh paint on the walls, and the general contractor was this guy named Rick who just yelled the whole time. He wore these bright yellow suspenders every single day and parked his obnoxious red truck right on the client's lawn. It had this huge dent in the bumper. \n\nBut yeah... I just feel like I deserve it. The punishment, I mean.\n\nDoctor: Are you hearing voices that other people can't hear?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I... I do hear someone. It's a man's voice. He usually sounds like he's right behind me, like, right over my shoulder. It happens when I'm at a job site, or even just standing in my kitchen at home. He says things like, \"You missed it,\" or \"Everyone knows.\" It's so clear, I always spin around thinking one of the guys snuck up on me. \n\nMy kitchen actually needs a lot of work. The tile is from the 70s, this awful mustard yellow with brown flowers. I've been meaning to rip it up and put down some nice luxury vinyl plank. It's waterproof, you know? Really durable. We installed it at this dentist's office last week. The dentist had these huge fish tanks in the waiting room, just massive. I kept worrying we'd accidentally bump one and flood the whole place. That would be a disaster. The water damage alone... \n\nBut yeah, every time I turn around in the kitchen, there's nobody there. Just me and that ugly yellow tile.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the voice is coming from a real person who is actually there?\n\nPatient: Like I just said, it sounds exactly like a real person. When I hear him say, \"You missed it,\" or \"Everyone knows,\" it is so clear, I swear someone is standing right there directly behind me. But then I turn around, and nobody's there. \n\nIt really messes with my head because it sounds just like one of the guys on the crew. We used to have this guy, Tommy, who would sneak up on people on site. He thought it was hilarious. He wore these heavy steel-toed boots but somehow he walked so quiet. Those boots are expensive, though. I usually get mine at this specialty workwear place out on Route 9. They have this digital machine that measures your arch, which is super important when you're on your feet all day on concrete subfloors. Concrete is just terrible for your joints. I've been wearing heavy-duty gel knee pads since I was in my twenties, but even then, the cold just seeps right through your jeans in the winter. We did a warehouse job in January once and my hands were so numb I could barely hold the utility knife to cut the seams. \n\nBut yeah... it sounds completely real until I look and see that I'm totally alone.\n\nDoctor: When you hear the man's voice, does it ever tell you that you deserve to die or be punished?\n\nPatient: No, um... no, he doesn't say that. It's just what I said a minute ago. I'll be in my kitchen or on a job site, and I hear him right behind me saying, \"You missed it,\" or \"Everyone knows.\" It's so clear, like he's standing right on my heels, but when I spin around... nobody. Just me. \n\nBut when he says \"everyone knows,\" it really gets under my skin. Because, you know, when you're laying floor and you mess up a seam, everyone *does* know. You can't hide a bad seam, especially not on a patterned broadloom. If the pattern match is off by even a fraction of an inch, the whole room looks crooked. We had this one client, this lady with a huge house out by the lake, who ordered this custom geometric carpet. Took eight weeks to ship from Europe. The rolls were unbelievably heavy. My lower back was just screaming by the end of the week. I actually had to start seeing a chiropractor after that job. Dr. Allen. His office is right next to that old bowling alley on 4th street. Have you ever been there? They still have those old manual scoring sheets and the carpet in there is just... well, it's a biohazard at this point. It's this neon galaxy print that hasn't been deep cleaned since 1998. The padding underneath must be completely disintegrated into dust by now. \n\nBut yeah, the voice just says those two things mostly. Not the punishment stuff.\n\nDoctor: What do you think the voice means when it says, \"Everyone knows\"?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I know exactly what it means. It's about the jobs. Even on the days when I'm not, you know, completely down in bed and crying all day... I just know it. I get this absolute certainty that the homeowners and the suppliers are all tracking me. They're using the floor patterns, the text messages, even the calls to the city for permits... they're using all of it to monitor whether I'm ruining the jobs on purpose. And my crew tries to tell me I'm crazy, but no one can talk me out of it because the proof is right there. \n\nLike the permits. Getting a permit pulled downtown is a nightmare. The building department is on the third floor of this brutalist concrete building that looks like a prison. The elevators never work. You have to walk up these stairs with these weird, shallow steps that totally throw off your stride. It's a trip hazard, honestly. If they had to pass their own safety inspection, they'd fail immediately. I took a picture of the stair nosing once to show my brother because he's a safety inspector for the county. He works mostly out in the rural areas, checking agricultural buildings. He had to go to a dairy farm last month and said the smell was just unbelievable. Stuck in his truck upholstery for a week. He drives this Ford F-150, the older model, before they switched to the aluminum bodies. The steel ones were so much better for hauling heavy loads, like rolls of carpet or bags of thinset. Thinset is incredibly heavy, fifty pounds a bag, and you need so many of them for a large tile job. \n\nBut yeah, they're definitely using all that stuff to track me. That's what he means. Everyone knows.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you were not depressed but still heard the man's voice?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, there was. Actually, just a couple of months ago, there was this stretch of, I don't know, several weeks where the fog kind of lifted a bit. I was actually getting out of bed without it feeling like lifting a hundred pounds, eating a little better, not just crying all day long. But... even then, I still heard him. I'd be at work, feeling okay, and there he was, right behind me. And I was still completely convinced that the clients and suppliers were tracking me. \n\nIt's funny how suppliers work, though. The guy who runs the counter, Stan, he's been there since the eighties. He still uses a dot-matrix printer for the invoices. Have you ever heard one of those things go off? It sounds like a machine gun. It's so loud you can't even hear yourself think. They have this coffee pot sitting right next to it that looks like it hasn't been washed since the Clinton administration. It's got this thick brown crust on the inside of the glass. I don't know how anyone drinks it, but the tile guys from the union shop down the street are always in there downing it by the quart. Those guys are crazy, though. They work at this frantic pace, just slapping down thinset like there's no tomorrow. You can't do that with large format tile. You get lippage, and then the whole floor is ruined. You have to back-butter every single piece and use those leveling clips, the little plastic wedges. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, like I was just saying... it was for several weeks. I wasn't depressed at all, really. I was getting up fine, eating decent, wasn't crying... but the voice was still right there with me at work, and I still knew for a fact that the clients and suppliers were tracking my every move. 

Suppliers are so sneaky like that. They have these barcode scanners now for inventory, the wireless ones that look like phasers from Star Trek. They scan the materials out to your account, but I know those scanners do more than just read barcodes. They can ping your phone's Bluetooth if you leave it on. I always tell my apprentices to turn off their Bluetooth when they go into the supply house, but kids these days, they always have those wireless earbuds in. They listen to the worst music, too. Just this heavy, repetitive bass. Boom, boom, boom. You can't even hear the snap of a chalk line over it. A good chalk line is essential, especially the Irwin ones with the blue chalk. The red chalk is permanent, you know. If you snap a red line on a porous stone tile by mistake, you've just bought that tile. It'll never wash out. We had a guy do that on a limestone foyer in this massive colonial house. The homeowner was this lawyer who wore these incredibly shiny shoes... 

But to answer your question, yes, it was at least a few weeks of feeling totally fine, mood-wise, but still hearing him and knowing I was being tracked.

No... no, I don't think so. I'm usually just so exhausted from the depression, or when that lifts, I'm just trying to catch up on sleep. I've never had a time where I felt super energized or didn't need to sleep. Sleep is huge in my trade, honestly. If you don't sleep, your knees feel it twice as bad the next day. 

Being a floor installer, you spend like eighty percent of your life on your knees. Even with the good kneepads—the ones with the gel inserts, not those cheap foam ones they sell at the big box stores—it still takes a toll. The gel ones are great, but they get really hot in the summer. You take them off at the end of the day and the back of your jeans is just soaked in sweat. It's disgusting. But if you don't wear them, you end up with bursitis, or "housemaid's knee" they call it. My old boss, Gary, he had to get his knee drained like three times a year. He was this huge guy, smoked two packs of Pall Malls a day. He always smelled like stale smoke and peppermint schnapps. He used to drive this old Chevy van that was completely rusted out on the bottom. You could literally see the road passing by beneath your feet if you sat in the passenger seat. We used to joke that we could Flintstone it if the engine ever died. 

But no, I've never felt unusually energized or anything like that. Just tired.

We had to bring in these heavy-duty scrapers, and the noise was just echoing down the halls. My brother used to help me with those jobs before he moved to Texas. He always said Texas had better barbecue, but I think he just wanted warmer winters. Anyway, yeah... I just feel like I deserve to be punished.\n\nDoctor: Are you thinking about killing yourself right now?\n\nPatient: Like I was saying, it's just... I keep thinking it would be so much better to just not wake up. I don't know if I'm about to do something right this exact second, but the thought doesn't really go away. I just really feel like dying is the only fair punishment for what I've put my family and my crew through. It's just this heavy weight on me all the time, you know? \n\nKind of like when we have to haul those massive rolls of broadloom carpet up the stairs. We had this one job last year at an old Victorian house they were converting into apartments, and the staircase was so narrow. Four of us just sweating and trying to pivot on the landing. I kept scuffing the fresh paint on the walls, and the general contractor was this guy named Rick who just yelled the whole time. He wore these bright yellow suspenders every single day and parked his obnoxious red truck right on the client's lawn. It had this huge dent in the bumper. \n\nBut yeah... I just feel like I deserve it. The punishment, I mean.\n\nDoctor: Are you hearing voices that other people can't hear?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I... I do hear someone. It's a man's voice. He usually sounds like he's right behind me, like, right over my shoulder. It happens when I'm at a job site, or even just standing in my kitchen at home. He says things like, \"You missed it,\" or \"Everyone knows.\" It's so clear, I always spin around thinking one of the guys snuck up on me. \n\nMy kitchen actually needs a lot of work. The tile is from the 70s, this awful mustard yellow with brown flowers. I've been meaning to rip it up and put down some nice luxury vinyl plank. It's waterproof, you know? Really durable. We installed it at this dentist's office last week. The dentist had these huge fish tanks in the waiting room, just massive. I kept worrying we'd accidentally bump one and flood the whole place. That would be a disaster. The water damage alone... \n\nBut yeah, every time I turn around in the kitchen, there's nobody there. Just me and that ugly yellow tile.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the voice is coming from a real person who is actually there?\n\nPatient: Like I just said, it sounds exactly like a real person. When I hear him say, \"You missed it,\" or \"Everyone knows,\" it is so clear, I swear someone is standing right there directly behind me. But then I turn around, and nobody's there. \n\nIt really messes with my head because it sounds just like one of the guys on the crew. We used to have this guy, Tommy, who would sneak up on people on site. He thought it was hilarious. He wore these heavy steel-toed boots but somehow he walked so quiet. Those boots are expensive, though. I usually get mine at this specialty workwear place out on Route 9. They have this digital machine that measures your arch, which is super important when you're on your feet all day on concrete subfloors. Concrete is just terrible for your joints. I've been wearing heavy-duty gel knee pads since I was in my twenties, but even then, the cold just seeps right through your jeans in the winter. We did a warehouse job in January once and my hands were so numb I could barely hold the utility knife to cut the seams. \n\nBut yeah... it sounds completely real until I look and see that I'm totally alone.\n\nDoctor: When you hear the man's voice, does it ever tell you that you deserve to die or be punished?\n\nPatient: No, um... no, he doesn't say that. It's just what I said a minute ago. I'll be in my kitchen or on a job site, and I hear him right behind me saying, \"You missed it,\" or \"Everyone knows.\" It's so clear, like he's standing right on my heels, but when I spin around... nobody. Just me. \n\nBut when he says \"everyone knows,\" it really gets under my skin. Because, you know, when you're laying floor and you mess up a seam, everyone *does* know. You can't hide a bad seam, especially not on a patterned broadloom. If the pattern match is off by even a fraction of an inch, the whole room looks crooked. We had this one client, this lady with a huge house out by the lake, who ordered this custom geometric carpet. Took eight weeks to ship from Europe. The rolls were unbelievably heavy. My lower back was just screaming by the end of the week. I actually had to start seeing a chiropractor after that job. Dr. Allen. His office is right next to that old bowling alley on 4th street. Have you ever been there? They still have those old manual scoring sheets and the carpet in there is just... well, it's a biohazard at this point. It's this neon galaxy print that hasn't been deep cleaned since 1998. The padding underneath must be completely disintegrated into dust by now. \n\nBut yeah, the voice just says those two things mostly. Not the punishment stuff.\n\nDoctor: What do you think the voice means when it says, \"Everyone knows\"?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I know exactly what it means. It's about the jobs. Even on the days when I'm not, you know, completely down in bed and crying all day... I just know it. I get this absolute certainty that the homeowners and the suppliers are all tracking me. They're using the floor patterns, the text messages, even the calls to the city for permits... they're using all of it to monitor whether I'm ruining the jobs on purpose. And my crew tries to tell me I'm crazy, but no one can talk me out of it because the proof is right there. \n\nLike the permits. Getting a permit pulled downtown is a nightmare. The building department is on the third floor of this brutalist concrete building that looks like a prison. The elevators never work. You have to walk up these stairs with these weird, shallow steps that totally throw off your stride. It's a trip hazard, honestly. If they had to pass their own safety inspection, they'd fail immediately. I took a picture of the stair nosing once to show my brother because he's a safety inspector for the county. He works mostly out in the rural areas, checking agricultural buildings. He had to go to a dairy farm last month and said the smell was just unbelievable. Stuck in his truck upholstery for a week. He drives this Ford F-150, the older model, before they switched to the aluminum bodies. The steel ones were so much better for hauling heavy loads, like rolls of carpet or bags of thinset. Thinset is incredibly heavy, fifty pounds a bag, and you need so many of them for a large tile job. \n\nBut yeah, they're definitely using all that stuff to track me. That's what he means. Everyone knows.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you were not depressed but still heard the man's voice?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, there was. Actually, just a couple of months ago, there was this stretch of, I don't know, several weeks where the fog kind of lifted a bit. I was actually getting out of bed without it feeling like lifting a hundred pounds, eating a little better, not just crying all day long. But... even then, I still heard him. I'd be at work, feeling okay, and there he was, right behind me. And I was still completely convinced that the clients and suppliers were tracking me. \n\nIt's funny how suppliers work, though. You'd think they'd want to make it easy for you to buy their stuff, but the supply house we use for the high-end adhesives is only open from 7 AM to 11 AM. It's ridiculous. The guy who runs the counter, Stan, he's been there since the eighties. He still uses a dot-matrix printer for the invoices. Have you ever heard one of those things go off? It sounds like a machine gun. It's so loud you can't even hear yourself think. They have this coffee pot sitting right next to it that looks like it hasn't been washed since the Clinton administration. It's got this thick brown crust on the inside of the glass. I don't know how anyone drinks it, but the tile guys from the union shop down the street are always in there downing it by the quart. Those guys are crazy, though. They work at this frantic pace, just slapping down thinset like there's no tomorrow. You can't do that with large format tile. You get lippage, and then the whole floor is ruined. You have to back-butter every single piece and use those leveling clips, the little plastic wedges. They leave little plastic bits everywhere when you snap them off the next day. \n\nBut yeah, even when I was feeling better and not so depressed, the voice was still there, and I still knew they were tracking me.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a time when you heard the voice and felt tracked for at least two weeks when you were not depressed at all?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I was just saying... it was for several weeks. I wasn't depressed at all, really. I was getting up fine, eating decent, wasn't crying... but the voice was still right there with me at work, and I still knew for a fact that the clients and suppliers were tracking my every move. \n\nSuppliers are so sneaky like that. They scan the materials out to your account, but I know those scanners do more than just read barcodes. They can ping your phone's Bluetooth if you leave it on. I always tell my apprentices to turn off their Bluetooth when they go into the supply house, but kids these days, they always have those wireless earbuds in. They listen to the worst music, too. Just this heavy, repetitive bass. Boom, boom, boom. You can't even hear the snap of a chalk line over it. A good chalk line is essential, especially the Irwin ones with the blue chalk. The red chalk is permanent, you know. If you snap a red line on a porous stone tile by mistake, you've just bought that tile. It'll never wash out. We had a guy do that on a limestone foyer in this massive colonial house. The homeowner was this lawyer who wore these incredibly shiny shoes... \n\nBut to answer your question, yes, it was at least a few weeks of feeling totally fine, mood-wise, but still hearing him and knowing I was being tracked.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt unusually energized and needed much less sleep?\n\nPatient: No... no, I don't think so. They have these barcode scanners now for inventory, the wireless ones that look like phasers from Star Trek. They scan the materials out to your account, but I know those scanners do more than just read barcodes. They can ping your phone's Bluetooth if you leave it on. I always tell my apprentices to turn off their Bluetooth when they go into the supply house, but kids these days, they always have those wireless earbuds in. They listen to the worst music, too. Just this heavy, repetitive bass. Boom, boom, boom. You can't even hear the snap of a chalk line over it. A good chalk line is essential, especially the Irwin ones with the blue chalk. The red chalk is permanent, you know. If you snap a red line on a porous stone tile by mistake, you've just bought that tile. It'll never wash out. We had a guy do that on a limestone foyer in this massive colonial house. The homeowner was this lawyer who wore these incredibly shiny shoes... \n\nBut to answer your question, yes, it was at least a few weeks of feeling totally fine, mood-wise, but still hearing him and knowing I was being tracked.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt unusually energized and needed much less sleep?\n\nPatient: No... no, I don't think so. I'm usually just so exhausted from the depression, or when that lifts, I'm just trying to catch up on sleep. I've never had a time where I felt super energized or didn't need to sleep. Sleep is huge in my trade, honestly. If you don't sleep, your knees feel it twice as bad the next day. \n\nBeing a floor installer, you spend like eighty percent of your life on your knees. Even with the good kneepads—the ones with the gel inserts, not those cheap foam ones they sell at the big box stores—it still takes a toll. The gel ones are great, but they get really hot in the summer. You take them off at the end of the day and the back of your jeans is just soaked in sweat. It's disgusting. But if you don't wear them, you end up with bursitis, or \"housemaid's knee\" they call it. My old boss, Gary, he had to get his knee drained like three times a year. He was this huge guy, smoked two packs of Pall Malls a day. He always smelled like stale smoke and peppermint schnapps. He used to drive this old Chevy van that was completely rusted out on the bottom. You could literally see the road passing by beneath your feet if you sat in the passenger seat. We used to joke that we could Flintstone it if the engine ever died. \n\nBut no, I've never felt unusually energized or anything like that. Just tired.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring those weeks when you were hearing the voice and felt tracked, were you completely free of depression?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, like I said, it was a stretch of several weeks where the depression just kind of lifted. I was getting up okay, eating a little better, not crying all day. But I still heard him at work, and I still stayed convinced that the clients and suppliers were tracking me. 

It's just crazy how much time you spend with clients, you know? You're in their house for days, sometimes weeks. You see everything. You see how they live, how messy they are. Some people are so particular, though. They want you to take your shoes off at the door, which I get, but I'm carrying eighty pounds of mortar, I can't be slipping around in my socks. They kept trying to eat the grout. Grout is toxic to dogs, I'm pretty sure. Or maybe it's just bad for their digestion. But anyway, yeah, I was completely free of the depression during those weeks, but all the other stuff was still happening.

That would be a disaster. The water damage alone... \n\nBut yeah, every time I turn around in the kitchen, there's nobody there. Just me and that ugly yellow tile.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the voice is coming from a real person who is actually there?\n\nPatient: Like I just said, it sounds exactly like a real person. When I hear him say, \"You missed it,\" or \"Everyone knows,\" it is so clear, I swear someone is standing right there directly behind me. But then I turn around, and nobody's there. \n\nIt really messes with my head because it sounds just like one of the guys on the crew. We used to have this guy, Tommy, who would sneak up on people on site. He thought it was hilarious. He wore these heavy steel-toed boots but somehow he walked so quiet. Those boots are expensive, though. I usually get mine at this specialty workwear place out on Route 9. They have this digital machine that measures your arch, which is super important when you're on your feet all day on concrete subfloors. Concrete is just terrible for your joints. I've been wearing heavy-duty gel knee pads since I was in my twenties, but even then, the cold just seeps right through your jeans in the winter. We did a warehouse job in January once and my hands were so numb I could barely hold the utility knife to cut the seams. \n\nBut yeah... it sounds completely real until I look and see that I'm totally alone.\n\nDoctor: When you hear the man's voice, does it ever tell you that you deserve to die or be punished?\n\nPatient: No, um... no, he doesn't say that. It's just what I said a minute ago. I'll be in my kitchen or on a job site, and I hear him right behind me saying, \"You missed it,\" or \"Everyone knows.\" It's so clear, like he's standing right on my heels, but when I spin around... nobody. Just me. \n\nBut when he says \"everyone knows,\" it really gets under my skin. Because, you know, when you're laying floor and you mess up a seam, everyone *does* know. You can't hide a bad seam, especially not on a patterned broadloom. If the pattern match is off by even a fraction of an inch, the whole room looks crooked. We had this one client, this lady with a huge house out by the lake, who ordered this custom geometric carpet. Took eight weeks to ship from Europe. The rolls were unbelievably heavy. My lower back was just screaming by the end of the week. I actually had to start seeing a chiropractor after that job. Dr. Allen. His office is right next to that old bowling alley on 4th street. Have you ever been there? They still have those old manual scoring sheets and the carpet in there is just... well, it's a biohazard at this point. It's this neon galaxy print that hasn't been deep cleaned since 1998. The padding underneath must be completely disintegrated into dust by now. \n\nBut yeah, the voice just says those two things mostly. Not the punishment stuff.\n\nDoctor: What do you think the voice means when it says, \"Everyone knows\"?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I know exactly what it means. It's about the jobs. Even on the days when I'm not, you know, completely down in bed and crying all day... I just know it. I get this absolute certainty that the homeowners and the suppliers are all tracking me. They're using the floor patterns, the text messages, even the calls to the city for permits... they're using all of it to monitor whether I'm ruining the jobs on purpose. And my crew tries to tell me I'm crazy, but no one can talk me out of it because the proof is right there. \n\nLike the permits. Getting a permit pulled downtown is a nightmare. The building department is on the third floor of this brutalist concrete building that looks like a prison. The elevators never work. You have to walk up these stairs with these weird, shallow steps that totally throw off your stride. It's a trip hazard, honestly. If they had to pass their own safety inspection, they'd fail immediately. I took a picture of the stair nosing once to show my brother because he's a safety inspector for the county. He works mostly out in the rural areas, checking agricultural buildings. He had to go to a dairy farm last month and said the smell was just unbelievable. Stuck in his truck upholstery for a week. He drives this Ford F-150, the older model, before they switched to the aluminum bodies. The steel ones were so much better for hauling heavy loads, like rolls of carpet or bags of thinset. Thinset is incredibly heavy, fifty pounds a bag, and you need so many of them for a large tile job. \n\nBut yeah, they're definitely using all that stuff to track me. That's what he means. Everyone knows.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you were not depressed but still heard the man's voice?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, there was. Actually, just a couple of months ago, there was this stretch of, I don't know, several weeks where the fog kind of lifted a bit. I was actually getting out of bed without it feeling like lifting a hundred pounds, eating a little better, not just crying all day long. But... even then, I still heard him. I'd be at work, feeling okay, and there he was, right behind me. And I was still completely convinced that the clients and suppliers were tracking me. \n\nIt's funny how suppliers work, though. You'd think they'd want to make it easy for you to buy their stuff, but the supply house we use for the high-end adhesives is only open from 7 AM to 11 AM. It's ridiculous. The guy who runs the counter, Stan, he's been there since the eighties. He still uses a dot-matrix printer for the invoices. Have you ever heard one of those things go off? It sounds like a machine gun. It's so loud you can't even hear yourself think. They have this coffee pot sitting right next to it that looks like it hasn't been washed since the Clinton administration. It's got this thick brown crust on the inside of the glass. I don't know how anyone drinks it, but the tile guys from the union shop down the street are always in there downing it by the quart. Those guys are crazy, though. They work at this frantic pace, just slapping down thinset like there's no tomorrow. You can't do that with large format tile. You get lippage, and then the whole floor is ruined. You have to back-butter every single piece and use those leveling clips, the little plastic wedges. They leave little plastic bits everywhere when you snap them off the next day. \n\nBut yeah, even when I was feeling better and not so depressed, the voice was still there, and I still knew they were tracking me.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a time when you heard the voice and felt tracked for at least two weeks when you were not depressed at all?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I was just saying... it was for several weeks. I wasn't depressed at all, really. I was getting up fine, eating decent, wasn't crying... but the voice was still right there with me at work, and I still knew for a fact that the clients and suppliers were tracking my every move. \n\nSuppliers are so sneaky like that. They have these barcode scanners now for inventory, the wireless ones that look like phasers from Star Trek. They scan the materials out to your account, but I know those scanners do more than just read barcodes. They can ping your phone's Bluetooth if you leave it on. I always tell my apprentices to turn off their Bluetooth when they go into the supply house, but kids these days, they always have those wireless earbuds in. They listen to the worst music, too. Just this heavy, repetitive bass. Boom, boom, boom. You can't even hear the snap of a chalk line over it. A good chalk line is essential, especially the Irwin ones with the blue chalk. The red chalk is permanent, you know. If you snap a red line on a porous stone tile by mistake, you've just bought that tile. It'll never wash out. We had a guy do that on a limestone foyer in this massive colonial house. The homeowner was this lawyer who wore these incredibly shiny shoes... \n\nBut to answer your question, yes, it was at least a few weeks of feeling totally fine, mood-wise, but still hearing him and knowing I was being tracked.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt unusually energized and needed much less sleep?\n\nPatient: No... no, I don't think so. I'm usually just so exhausted from the depression, or when that lifts, I'm just trying to catch up on sleep. I've never had a time where I felt super energized or didn't need to sleep. Sleep is huge in my trade, honestly. If you don't sleep, your knees feel it twice as bad the next day. \n\nBeing a floor installer, you spend like eighty percent of your life on your knees. Even with the good kneepads—the ones with the gel inserts, not those cheap foam ones they sell at the big box stores—it still takes a toll. The gel ones are great, but they get really hot in the summer. You take them off at the end of the day and the back of your jeans is just soaked in sweat. It's disgusting. But if you don't wear them, you end up with bursitis, or \"housemaid's knee\" they call it. My old boss, Gary, he had to get his knee drained like three times a year. He was this huge guy, smoked two packs of Pall Malls a day. He always smelled like stale smoke and peppermint schnapps. He used to drive this old Chevy van that was completely rusted out on the bottom. You could literally see the road passing by beneath your feet if you sat in the passenger seat. We used to joke that we could Flintstone it if the engine ever died. \n\nBut no, I've never felt unusually energized or anything like that. Just tired.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks when you were hearing the voice and felt tracked, were you completely free of depression?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I said, it was a stretch of several weeks where the depression just kind of lifted. I was getting up okay, eating a little better, not crying all day. But I still heard him at work, and I still stayed convinced that the clients and suppliers were tracking me. \n\nIt's just crazy how much time you spend with clients, you know? You're in their house for days, sometimes weeks. You see everything. You see how they live, how messy they are. Some people are so particular, though. They want you to take your shoes off at the door, which I get, but I'm carrying eighty pounds of mortar, I can't be slipping around in my socks. We had this one lady, she wanted us to put down this special rosin paper on every single surface we walked on, even the driveway. Do you know how much rosin paper costs? It's not cheap. And it tears so easily if you turn your foot the wrong way. We spent more time taping down paper than we did actually laying the floor. She had these little yappy dogs, too, Pomeranians or something. They kept trying to eat the grout. Grout is toxic to dogs, I'm pretty sure. Or maybe it's just bad for their digestion. But anyway, yeah, I was completely free of the depression during those weeks, but all the other stuff was still happening.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, completely. It's like... everything just drains out of me. I used to do so much. I used to paint, you know, watercolors mostly. And I'd cook these big meals, like real involved stuff with homemade pasta and sauces that take all day to simmer. I'd even go running before heading out to a job site, which sounds crazy now because flooring is such a physical job. And I was in this community theater group, too. We did a lot of Neil Simon plays. 

Community theater is wild, though. The drama off-stage is always ten times worse than what's happening on stage. There was this one guy, Richard, he always got the lead roles just because he owned the local hardware store and donated all the lumber for the sets. He couldn't act to save his life. He always forgot his lines and would just stare out into the audience with this deer-in-the-headlights look. And his wife, she did the costumes, and she would always make the younger girls' dresses just a little too tight, I swear she did it on purpose. She used this awful cheap polyester that didn't breathe at all. Under those stage lights, you'd be sweating buckets. Stage lights are no joke. They use these old halogen bulbs that get so hot they can actually start a fire if you put a gel too close to them. A gel is this colored plastic sheet they use to change the color of the light. We had a blue one melt once right in the middle of a scene... 

But yeah, now? All that stuff—the painting, the cooking, the running, the theater—it just feels like chores from somebody else's life. I don't even care if I miss rehearsals or whatever. I just have zero interest in any of it.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I came in because... well, lately I keep thinking it would just be better if I didn't wake up. Like, going to sleep and just being done. Sometimes I feel like dying would be the fair punishment for what I've done to my family and my crew at work. We were doing this huge commercial job last month, pulling up all this old linoleum in a hospital wing. The glue they used back in the day, it's like cement, I swear. He always said Texas had better barbecue, but I think he just wanted warmer winters. Anyway, yeah... We used to have this guy, Tommy, who would sneak up on people on site. He thought it was hilarious. He wore these heavy steel-toed boots but somehow he walked so quiet. They have this digital machine that measures your arch, which is super important when you're on your feet all day on concrete subfloors. Concrete is just terrible for your joints. I've been wearing heavy-duty gel knee pads since I was in my twenties, but even then, the cold just seeps right through your jeans in the winter. We did a warehouse job in January once and my hands were so numb I could barely hold the utility knife to cut the seams. \n\nBut yeah... it sounds completely real until I look and see that I'm totally alone.\n\nDoctor: When you hear the man's voice, does it ever tell you that you deserve to die or be punished?\n\nPatient: No, um... no, he doesn't say that. It's just what I said a minute ago. I'll be in my kitchen or on a job site, and I hear him right behind me saying, \"You missed it,\" or \"Everyone knows.\" It's so clear, like he's standing right on my heels, but when I spin around... nobody. Just me. \n\nBut when he says \"everyone knows,\" it really gets under my skin. Because, you know, when you're laying floor and you mess up a seam, everyone *does* know. You can't hide a bad seam, especially not on a patterned broadloom. If the pattern match is off by even a fraction of an inch, the whole room looks crooked. We had this one client, this lady with a huge house out by the lake, who ordered this custom geometric carpet. Took eight weeks to ship from Europe. The rolls were unbelievably heavy. My lower back was just screaming by the end of the week. I actually had to start seeing a chiropractor after that job. Dr. Allen. His office is right next to that old bowling alley on 4th street. Have you ever been there? They still have those old manual scoring sheets and the carpet in there is just... well, it's a biohazard at this point. It's this neon galaxy print that hasn't been deep cleaned since 1998. The padding underneath must be completely disintegrated into dust by now. \n\nBut yeah, the voice just says those two things mostly. Not the punishment stuff.\n\nDoctor: What do you think the voice means when it says, \"Everyone knows\"?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I know exactly what it means. It's about the jobs. Even on the days when I'm not, you know, completely down in bed and crying all day... I just know it. I get this absolute certainty that the homeowners and the suppliers are all tracking me. They're using the floor patterns, the text messages, even the calls to the city for permits... they're using all of it to monitor whether I'm ruining the jobs on purpose. And my crew tries to tell me I'm crazy, but no one can talk me out of it because the proof is right there. \n\nLike the permits. Getting a permit pulled downtown is a nightmare. The building department is on the third floor of this brutalist concrete building that looks like a prison. The elevators never work. You have to walk up these stairs with these weird, shallow steps that totally throw off your stride. It's a trip hazard, honestly. If they had to pass their own safety inspection, they'd fail immediately. I took a picture of the stair nosing once to show my brother because he's a safety inspector for the county. He works mostly out in the rural areas, checking agricultural buildings. He had to go to a dairy farm last month and said the smell was just unbelievable. Stuck in his truck upholstery for a week. He drives this Ford F-150, the older model, before they switched to the aluminum bodies. The steel ones were so much better for hauling heavy loads, like rolls of carpet or bags of thinset. Thinset is incredibly heavy, fifty pounds a bag, and you need so many of them for a large tile job. \n\nBut yeah, they're definitely using all that stuff to track me. That's what he means. Everyone knows.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you were not depressed but still heard the man's voice?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, there was. Actually, just a couple of months ago, there was this stretch of, I don't know, several weeks where the fog kind of lifted a bit. I was actually getting out of bed without it feeling like lifting a hundred pounds, eating a little better, not just crying all day long. But... even then, I still heard him. I'd be at work, feeling okay, and there he was, right behind me. And I was still completely convinced that the clients and suppliers were tracking me. \n\nIt's funny how suppliers work, though. You'd think they'd want to make it easy for you to buy their stuff, but the supply house we use for the high-end adhesives is only open from 7 AM to 11 AM. It's ridiculous. The guy who runs the counter, Stan, he's been there since the eighties. He still uses a dot-matrix printer for the invoices. Have you ever heard one of those things go off? It sounds like a machine gun. It's so loud you can't even hear yourself think. They have this coffee pot sitting right next to it that looks like it hasn't been washed since the Clinton administration. It's got this thick brown crust on the inside of the glass. I don't know how anyone drinks it, but the tile guys from the union shop down the street are always in there downing it by the quart. Those guys are crazy, though. They work at this frantic pace, just slapping down thinset like there's no tomorrow. You can't do that with large format tile. You get lippage, and then the whole floor is ruined. You have to back-butter every single piece and use those leveling clips, the little plastic wedges. They leave little plastic bits everywhere when you snap them off the next day. \n\nBut yeah, even when I was feeling better and not so depressed, the voice was still there, and I still knew they were tracking me.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a time when you heard the voice and felt tracked for at least two weeks when you were not depressed at all?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I was just saying... it was for several weeks. I wasn't depressed at all, really. I was getting up fine, eating decent, wasn't crying... but the voice was still right there with me at work, and I still knew for a fact that the clients and suppliers were tracking my every move. \n\nSuppliers are so sneaky like that. They have these barcode scanners now for inventory, the wireless ones that look like phasers from Star Trek. They scan the materials out to your account, but I know those scanners do more than just read barcodes. They can ping your phone's Bluetooth if you leave it on. I always tell my apprentices to turn off their Bluetooth when they go into the supply house, but kids these days, they always have those wireless earbuds in. They listen to the worst music, too. Just this heavy, repetitive bass. Boom, boom, boom. You can't even hear the snap of a chalk line over it. A good chalk line is essential, especially the Irwin ones with the blue chalk. The red chalk is permanent, you know. If you snap a red line on a porous stone tile by mistake, you've just bought that tile. It'll never wash out. We had a guy do that on a limestone foyer in this massive colonial house. The homeowner was this lawyer who wore these incredibly shiny shoes... \n\nBut to answer your question, yes, it was at least a few weeks of feeling totally fine, mood-wise, but still hearing him and knowing I was being tracked.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt unusually energized and needed much less sleep?\n\nPatient: No... no, I don't think so. I'm usually just so exhausted from the depression, or when that lifts, I'm just trying to catch up on sleep. I've never had a time where I felt super energized or didn't need to sleep. Sleep is huge in my trade, honestly. If you don't sleep, your knees feel it twice as bad the next day. \n\nBeing a floor installer, you spend like eighty percent of your life on your knees. Even with the good kneepads—the ones with the gel inserts, not those cheap foam ones they sell at the big box stores—it still takes a toll. The gel ones are great, but they get really hot in the summer. You take them off at the end of the day and the back of your jeans is just soaked in sweat. It's disgusting. But if you don't wear them, you end up with bursitis, or \"housemaid's knee\" they call it. My old boss, Gary, he had to get his knee drained like three times a year. He was this huge guy, smoked two packs of Pall Malls a day. He always smelled like stale smoke and peppermint schnapps. He used to drive this old Chevy van that was completely rusted out on the bottom. You could literally see the road passing by beneath your feet if you sat in the passenger seat. We used to joke that we could Flintstone it if the engine ever died. \n\nBut no, I've never felt unusually energized or anything like that. Just tired.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks when you were hearing the voice and felt tracked, were you completely free of depression?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I said, it was a stretch of several weeks where the depression just kind of lifted. I was getting up okay, eating a little better, not crying all day. But I still heard him at work, and I still stayed convinced that the clients and suppliers were tracking me. \n\nIt's just crazy how much time you spend with clients, you know? You're in their house for days, sometimes weeks. You see everything. You see how they live, how messy they are. Some people are so particular, though. They want you to take your shoes off at the door, which I get, but I'm carrying eighty pounds of mortar, I can't be slipping around in my socks. We had this one lady, she wanted us to put down this special rosin paper on every single surface we walked on, even the driveway. Do you know how much rosin paper costs? It's not cheap. And it tears so easily if you turn your foot the wrong way. We spent more time taping down paper than we did actually laying the floor. She had these little yappy dogs, too, Pomeranians or something. They kept trying to eat the grout. Grout is toxic to dogs, I'm pretty sure. Or maybe it's just bad for their digestion. But anyway, yeah, I was completely free of the depression during those weeks, but all the other stuff was still happening.\n\nDoctor: During those down periods, did you lose interest or pleasure in things you usually care about?\n\nPatient: Yeah, completely. It's like... everything just drains out of me. I used to do so much. I used to paint, you know, watercolors mostly. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, it really does. Most days I just completely forget to eat until it's super late, or if I do try to force something down, I take a few bites and just feel sick to my stomach. It's gotten to the point where my work pants have gotten really loose, and I'm not even trying to lose weight. 

It's actually really annoying because finding good work pants for women is basically impossible to begin with. You either get the ones that fit okay but have those useless fake pockets, or you have to buy men's pants and they just don't sit right on your hips. I bought this one pair from Duluth a while back, those thick fire hose ones that are supposed to be indestructible? I ripped a hole in the knee on day two. Snagged it on a tack strip. Tack strips are just evil, honestly. They have all these little nails pointing straight up, and if you slip and catch your knuckle on one, it just shreds your skin. You bleed everywhere. And obviously, you can't bleed on a client's brand new white plush carpet. We always keep a tube of superglue in the truck just to seal up cuts so we can keep working. I read somewhere that superglue was actually invented for the military, like for medics in Vietnam to close wounds fast on the battlefield. It burns like absolute fire when you put it on a fresh cut, though. You have to blow on it for like five minutes straight. 

But anyway... yeah, my pants are just falling down on me now because I don't have an appetite.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I came in because... well, lately I keep thinking it would just be better if I didn't wake up. Like, going to sleep and just being done. Sometimes I feel like dying would be the fair punishment for what I've done to my family and my crew at work. We were doing this huge commercial job last month, pulling up all this old linoleum in a hospital wing. The glue they used back in the day, it's like cement, I swear. We had to bring in these heavy-duty scrapers, and the noise was just echoing down the halls. My brother used to help me with those jobs before he moved to Texas. He always said Texas had better barbecue, but I think he just wanted warmer winters. Anyway, yeah... I just feel like I deserve to be punished.\n\nDoctor: Are you thinking about killing yourself right now?\n\nPatient: Like I was saying, it's just... I keep thinking it would be so much better to just not wake up. I don't know if I'm about to do something right this exact second, but the thought doesn't really go away. I just really feel like dying is the only fair punishment for what I've put my family and my crew through. It's just this heavy weight on me all the time, you know? \n\nKind of like when we have to haul those massive rolls of broadloom carpet up the stairs. We had this one job last year at an old Victorian house they were converting into apartments, and the staircase was so narrow. Four of us just sweating and trying to pivot on the landing. I kept scuffing the fresh paint on the walls, and the general contractor was this guy named Rick who just yelled the whole time. He wore these bright yellow suspenders every single day and parked his obnoxious red truck right on the client's lawn. It had this huge dent in the bumper. \n\nBut yeah... I just feel like I deserve it. The punishment, I mean.\n\nDoctor: Are you hearing voices that other people can't hear?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I... I do hear someone. It's a man's voice. He usually sounds like he's right behind me, like, right over my shoulder. It happens when I'm at a job site, or even just standing in my kitchen at home. He says things like, \"You missed it,\" or \"Everyone knows.\" It's so clear, I always spin around thinking one of the guys snuck up on me. \n\nMy kitchen actually needs a lot of work. The tile is from the 70s, this awful mustard yellow with brown flowers. I've been meaning to rip it up and put down some nice luxury vinyl plank. It's waterproof, you know? Really durable. We installed it at this dentist's office last week. The dentist had these huge fish tanks in the waiting room, just massive. I kept worrying we'd accidentally bump one and flood the whole place. That would be a disaster. The water damage alone... \n\nBut yeah, every time I turn around in the kitchen, there's nobody there. Just me and that ugly yellow tile.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the voice is coming from a real person who is actually there?\n\nPatient: Like I just said, it sounds exactly like a real person. When I hear him say, \"You missed it,\" or \"Everyone knows,\" it is so clear, I swear someone is standing right there directly behind me. But then I turn around, and nobody's there. \n\nIt really messes with my head because it sounds just like one of the guys on the crew. We used to have this guy, Tommy, who would sneak up on people on site. He thought it was hilarious. He wore these heavy steel-toed boots but somehow he walked so quiet. Those boots are expensive, though. I usually get mine at this specialty workwear place out on Route 9. They have this digital machine that measures your arch, which is super important when you're on your feet all day on concrete subfloors. Concrete is just terrible for your joints. I've been wearing heavy-duty gel knee pads since I was in my twenties, but even then, the cold just seeps right through your jeans in the winter. We did a warehouse job in January once and my hands were so numb I could barely hold the utility knife to cut the seams. \n\nBut yeah... it sounds completely real until I look and see that I'm totally alone.\n\nDoctor: When you hear the man's voice, does it ever tell you that you deserve to die or be punished?\n\nPatient: No, um... no, he doesn't say that. It's just what I said a minute ago. I'll be in my kitchen or on a job site, and I hear him right behind me saying, \"You missed it,\" or \"Everyone knows.\" It's so clear, like he's standing right on my heels, but when I spin around... nobody. Just me. \n\nBut when he says \"everyone knows,\" it really gets under my skin. Because, you know, when you're laying floor and you mess up a seam, everyone *does* know. You can't hide a bad seam, especially not on a patterned broadloom. If the pattern match is off by even a fraction of an inch, the whole room looks crooked. We had this one client, this lady with a huge house out by the lake, who ordered this custom geometric carpet. Took eight weeks to ship from Europe. The rolls were unbelievably heavy. My lower back was just screaming by the end of the week. I actually had to start seeing a chiropractor after that job. Dr. Allen. His office is right next to that old bowling alley on 4th street. Have you ever been there? They still have those old manual scoring sheets and the carpet in there is just... well, it's a biohazard at this point. It's this neon galaxy print that hasn't been deep cleaned since 1998. The padding underneath must be completely disintegrated into dust by now. \n\nBut yeah, the voice just says those two things mostly. Not the punishment stuff.\n\nDoctor: What do you think the voice means when it says, \"Everyone knows\"?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I know exactly what it means. It's about the jobs. Even on the days when I'm not, you know, completely down in bed and crying all day... I just know it. I get this absolute certainty that the homeowners and the suppliers are all tracking me. They're using the floor patterns, the text messages, even the calls to the city for permits... they're using all of it to monitor whether I'm ruining the jobs on purpose. And my crew tries to tell me I'm crazy, but no one can talk me out of it because the proof is right there. \n\nLike the permits. Getting a permit pulled downtown is a nightmare. The building department is on the third floor of this brutalist concrete building that looks like a prison. The elevators never work. You have to walk up these stairs with these weird, shallow steps that totally throw off your stride. It's a trip hazard, honestly. If they had to pass their own safety inspection, they'd fail immediately. I took a picture of the stair nosing once to show my brother because he's a safety inspector for the county. He works mostly out in the rural areas, checking agricultural buildings. He had to go to a dairy farm last month and said the smell was just unbelievable. Stuck in his truck upholstery for a week. He drives this Ford F-150, the older model, before they switched to the aluminum bodies. The steel ones were so much better for hauling heavy loads, like rolls of carpet or bags of thinset. Thinset is incredibly heavy, fifty pounds a bag, and you need so many of them for a large tile job. \n\nBut yeah, they're definitely using all that stuff to track me. That's what he means. Everyone knows.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you were not depressed but still heard the man's voice?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, there was. Actually, just a couple of months ago, there was this stretch of, I don't know, several weeks where the fog kind of lifted a bit. I was actually getting out of bed without it feeling like lifting a hundred pounds, eating a little better, not just crying all day long. But... even then, I still heard him. I'd be at work, feeling okay, and there he was, right behind me. And I was still completely convinced that the clients and suppliers were tracking me. \n\nIt's funny how suppliers work, though. You'd think they'd want to make it easy for you to buy their stuff, but the supply house we use for the high-end adhesives is only open from 7 AM to 11 AM. It's ridiculous. The guy who runs the counter, Stan, he's been there since the eighties. He still uses a dot-matrix printer for the invoices. Have you ever heard one of those things go off? It sounds like a machine gun. It's so loud you can't even hear yourself think. They have this coffee pot sitting right next to it that looks like it hasn't been washed since the Clinton administration. It's got this thick brown crust on the inside of the glass. I don't know how anyone drinks it, but the tile guys from the union shop down the street are always in there downing it by the quart. Those guys are crazy, though. They work at this frantic pace, just slapping down thinset like there's no tomorrow. You can't do that with large format tile. You get lippage, and then the whole floor is ruined. You have to back-butter every single piece and use those leveling clips, the little plastic wedges. They leave little plastic bits everywhere when you snap them off the next day. \n\nBut yeah, even when I was feeling better and not so depressed, the voice was still there, and I still knew they were tracking me.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a time when you heard the voice and felt tracked for at least two weeks when you were not depressed at all?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I was just saying... it was for several weeks. I wasn't depressed at all, really. I was getting up fine, eating decent, wasn't crying... but the voice was still right there with me at work, and I still knew for a fact that the clients and suppliers were tracking my every move. \n\nSuppliers are so sneaky like that. They have these barcode scanners now for inventory, the wireless ones that look like phasers from Star Trek. They scan the materials out to your account, but I know those scanners do more than just read barcodes. They can ping your phone's Bluetooth if you leave it on. I always tell my apprentices to turn off their Bluetooth when they go into the supply house, but kids these days, they always have those wireless earbuds in. They listen to the worst music, too. Just this heavy, repetitive bass. Boom, boom, boom. You can't even hear the snap of a chalk line over it. A good chalk line is essential, especially the Irwin ones with the blue chalk. The red chalk is permanent, you know. If you snap a red line on a porous stone tile by mistake, you've just bought that tile. It'll never wash out. We had a guy do that on a limestone foyer in this massive colonial house. The homeowner was this lawyer who wore these incredibly shiny shoes... \n\nBut to answer your question, yes, it was at least a few weeks of feeling totally fine, mood-wise, but still hearing him and knowing I was being tracked.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt unusually energized and needed much less sleep?\n\nPatient: No... no, I don't think so. I'm usually just so exhausted from the depression, or when that lifts, I'm just trying to catch up on sleep. I've never had a time where I felt super energized or didn't need to sleep. Sleep is huge in my trade, honestly. If you don't sleep, your knees feel it twice as bad the next day. \n\nBeing a floor installer, you spend like eighty percent of your life on your knees. Even with the good kneepads—the ones with the gel inserts, not those cheap foam ones they sell at the big box stores—it still takes a toll. The gel ones are great, but they get really hot in the summer. You take them off at the end of the day and the back of your jeans is just soaked in sweat. It's disgusting. But if you don't wear them, you end up with bursitis, or \"housemaid's knee\" they call it. My old boss, Gary, he had to get his knee drained like three times a year. He was this huge guy, smoked two packs of Pall Malls a day. He always smelled like stale smoke and peppermint schnapps. He used to drive this old Chevy van that was completely rusted out on the bottom. You could literally see the road passing by beneath your feet if you sat in the passenger seat. We used to joke that we could Flintstone it if the engine ever died. \n\nBut no, I've never felt unusually energized or anything like that. Just tired.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks when you were hearing the voice and felt tracked, were you completely free of depression?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I said, it was a stretch of several weeks where the depression just kind of lifted. I was getting up okay, eating a little better, not crying all day. But I still heard him at work, and I still stayed convinced that the clients and suppliers were tracking me. \n\nIt's just crazy how much time you spend with clients, you know? You're in their house for days, sometimes weeks. You see everything. You see how they live, how messy they are. Some people are so particular, though. They want you to take your shoes off at the door, which I get, but I'm carrying eighty pounds of mortar, I can't be slipping around in my socks. We had this one lady, she wanted us to put down this special rosin paper on every single surface we walked on, even the driveway. Do you know how much rosin paper costs? It's not cheap. And it tears so easily if you turn your foot the wrong way. We spent more time taping down paper than we did actually laying the floor. She had these little yappy dogs, too, Pomeranians or something. They kept trying to eat the grout. Grout is toxic to dogs, I'm pretty sure. Or maybe it's just bad for their digestion. But anyway, yeah, I was completely free of the depression during those weeks, but all the other stuff was still happening.\n\nDoctor: During those down periods, did you lose interest or pleasure in things you usually care about?\n\nPatient: Yeah, completely. It's like... everything just drains out of me. I used to do so much. I used to paint, you know, watercolors mostly. And I'd cook these big meals, like real involved stuff with homemade pasta and sauces that take all day to simmer. I'd even go running before heading out to a job site, which sounds crazy now because flooring is such a physical job. And I was in this community theater group, too. We did a lot of Neil Simon plays. \n\nCommunity theater is wild, though. The drama off-stage is always ten times worse than what's happening on stage. There was this one guy, Richard, he always got the lead roles just because he owned the local hardware store and donated all the lumber for the sets. He couldn't act to save his life. He always forgot his lines and would just stare out into the audience with this deer-in-the-headlights look. And his wife, she did the costumes, and she would always make the younger girls' dresses just a little too tight, I swear she did it on purpose. She used this awful cheap polyester that didn't breathe at all. Under those stage lights, you'd be sweating buckets. Stage lights are no joke. They use these old halogen bulbs that get so hot they can actually start a fire if you put a gel too close to them. A gel is this colored plastic sheet they use to change the color of the light. We had a blue one melt once right in the middle of a scene... \n\nBut yeah, now? All that stuff—the painting, the cooking, the running, the theater—it just feels like chores from somebody else's life. I don't even care if I miss rehearsals or whatever. I just have zero interest in any of it.\n\nDoctor: During those down periods, did your appetite change a lot?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really does. Most days I just completely forget to eat until it's super late, or if I do try to force something down, I take a few bites and just feel sick to my stomach. It's gotten to the point where my work pants have gotten really loose, and I'm not even trying to lose weight. \n\nIt's actually really annoying because finding good work pants for women is basically impossible to begin with. You either get the ones that fit okay but have those useless fake pockets, or you have to buy men's pants and they just don't sit right on your hips. I bought this one pair from Duluth a while back, those thick fire hose ones that are supposed to be indestructible? I ripped a hole in the knee on day two. Snagged it on a tack strip. Tack strips are just evil, honestly. They have all these little nails pointing straight up, and if you slip and catch your knuckle on one, it just shreds your skin. You bleed everywhere. And obviously, you can't bleed on a client's brand new white plush carpet. We always keep a tube of superglue in the truck just to seal up cuts so we can keep working. I read somewhere that superglue was actually invented for the military, like for medics in Vietnam to close wounds fast on the battlefield. It burns like absolute fire when you put it on a fresh cut, though. You have to blow on it for like five minutes straight. \n\nBut anyway... yeah, my pants are just falling down on me now because I don't have an appetite.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
